		<item>
		<title>Securing AI agents with temporal policies in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/securing-ai-agents-with-temporal-policies-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sean Eichenberger]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 06 Aug 2026 18:57:55 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">6eddb7db47dabb7927abcbbe6200579df3a4f157</guid>

					<description>Temporal policies in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore let you define stateful rules that evaluate authorization based on an agent's session history. Learn how to enforce workflow sequencing, prevent data fabrication, cap financial exposure, and require human approval for high-value actions.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Before AI agents, it was generally sufficient for access controls to treat each action as an independent event. Applications relied on deterministic business logic to enforce whether actions happened in the right order or whether the data was up-to-date. AI agents behave in fundamentally different ways than traditional applications. They decide at runtime which tools to call, with which arguments, and in what order. That flexibility, combined with increasingly intelligent models, makes agents equal measures capable and challenging to control. One tool call might be deemed safe when considered in isolation, but harmful in the context of the preceding call, such as after reading from an untrusted data source. The question then becomes, how do you enforce authorization rules that account for an agent’s session history, in a way the agent cannot circumvent?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-temporal.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Temporal policies&lt;/a&gt; in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; let you define stateful rules that determine authorization to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt; targets by evaluating the current request in the context of prior events in an agent’s trajectory. Because these policies run at the AgentCore Gateway perimeter, outside the agent’s own code, the agent cannot intercept or manipulate them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you will learn what temporal policies are, how they work, and walk through an example to demonstrate. We will show you how to use temporal policies to enforce workflow sequencing, prevent data fabrication between tool calls, cap cumulative financial exposure per session, and require human approval for high-value actions. You will also see how to automatically tighten permissions when an agent operates without human engagement. First, however, we will explore the needs and use cases for stateful policies in more detail.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-agents-need-stateful-policy-enforcement"&gt;Why agents need stateful policy enforcement&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Existing access controls in AgentCore Policy enforce stateless, deterministic rules on each individual request: who can call which tool, under what conditions. Stateless controls are necessary but often insufficient for agents. Consider the following scenarios where existing stateless controls fail to catch critical issues:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An agent calls a &lt;code&gt;lookup_customer&lt;/code&gt; tool, hallucinates a different account number than what was returned, and passes it to a &lt;code&gt;transfer_funds&lt;/code&gt; tool that then moves money to the wrong customer’s account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A runaway agent executes dozens of trades in a loop because nothing tracks that cumulative exposure has already exceeded the risk limit.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An agent both approves and denies the same insurance claim within seconds.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each individual tool call in these scenarios would pass a stateless policy check. The problem only becomes apparent when you look at the agent’s trajectory, the ordered sequence of actions in a session. Temporal policies extend Policy in AgentCore with this trajectory-aware enforcement layer. Temporal policies run at the gateway, outside the agent’s code, so they cannot be bypassed regardless of what the agent does, how it is prompted, or what bugs exist in the agent code. Some common temporal policy use cases include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enforcing output integrity across chained tools.&lt;/strong&gt; Require that an argument passed to the current tool call exactly matches the output of a prior tool call, preventing the agent from hallucinating or substituting values between steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enforcing tool-call ordering.&lt;/strong&gt; Require that one tool is called before another tool to verify standard operating procedure (SOP) adherence.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Requiring human approval before privileged actions.&lt;/strong&gt; Block destructive or sensitive tool calls until an explicit human approval event is recorded in the trajectory.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enforcing data freshness.&lt;/strong&gt; Require that a data lookup completed within a given timeframe before a dependent action is authorized, preventing decisions based on stale information.Temporal policies are authorization controls that answer the question “given the recent trajectory observed at the AgentCore Gateway, is this specific request authorized?”. They evaluate whether a gateway-routed request should be permitted based on the current request &lt;em&gt;and&lt;/em&gt; recent trajectory (that is, events within a session). They do not transform requests, call tools, perform analysis, or directly orchestrate the agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Temporal policies operate on the traffic that flows through AgentCore Gateway. Because Gateway routes an agent’s Model Context Protocol (MCP) tool calls, agent-to-agent calls, and model inference calls through a single endpoint, a temporal policy can govern all three whenever your agent issues those calls through the gateway. This gives you one consistent place to reason about an agent’s behavior over time, regardless of which kind of call the agent is making.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-temporal-policies-work"&gt;How temporal policies work&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Temporal policies build on the existing policy engine that’s already used for stateless access control. They introduce the concept of agent trajectories, which are bounded sequences of actions identified by a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-core-concepts.html#concept-principal-types" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;principal&lt;/a&gt; and session ID. Agents never see the policy logic, never touch the state store, and cannot alter the controls. As with the existing AgentCore Policy features, temporal policies deny by default and forbid wins over permit.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the gateway receives a tool call, the policy engine:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Queries the trajectory state for actions, inputs, and outputs relevant to the policies being evaluated.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Evaluates each temporal policy against the current request in the context of its historical scope (that is, prior events within the customer-defined trajectory).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Returns a deterministic ALLOW or DENY decision and logs the full context of the decision.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every request that a temporal policy evaluates must carry an &lt;code&gt;x-amzn-bedrock-agentcore-policy-session-id&lt;/code&gt; header, which identifies the session the request belongs to. You decide what constitutes the beginning and end of a session. The boundary can reflect whatever unit of work makes sense for your application, whether that is a single user conversation, a multi-step task, or a longer-running workflow. Because there can be no more than one concurrent authorization request per session, we recommend keeping the scope of a session as narrow as possible. If no header is passed, one will be generated on your behalf. However, note that a new session ID means that the policy engine will evaluate against a new, empty trajectory with no history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A session is never defined by its ID alone. AgentCore combines the session ID with the end user’s identity to produce a unique session, which means two different identities can present the same session ID and still be treated as having entirely separate sessions. Policies apply independently to each trajectory, because the underlying identity differs. Within an active session, agent trajectories carry a maximum look-back window of 24 hours. Any trajectory events older than that are automatically deleted. One additional rule governs the relationship between sessions and the policies themselves. Whenever a change is made to the policies in a policy engine, existing sessions are invalidated. This makes sure that each session is evaluated against the current set of policies and each relevant trajectory event is recorded with the expected schema.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="applying-temporal-policies-to-a-private-banking-portfolio-agent"&gt;Applying temporal policies to a private banking portfolio agent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To make these concepts concrete, we’ll walk through how temporal policies can secure a hypothetical private banking agent. The agent helps wealth advisors at a financial services firm manage client portfolios. It retrieves client profiles, loads portfolio holdings, fetches real-time market prices, performs analysis, and executes trades on the advisor’s behalf.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this scenario, the following MCP tools are exposed through the AgentCore Gateway:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;get_client_profile&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Retrieves client’s risk tolerance, investment policy, account restrictions, and associated portfolio IDs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;load_portfolio&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Retrieves a client’s portfolio holdings and current positions&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;get_market_price&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Fetches current market price for a security&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;execute_trade&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Executes a buy or sell order against a portfolio&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;rebalance_portfolio&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Adjusts portfolio allocations across holdings&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are three different advisor roles: junior advisors (limited trade authority), senior advisors (full trade authority), and compliance officers (read-only monitoring access). In this example, we will use &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cognito/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Cognito&lt;/a&gt; for identity and pass JWTs for inbound auth to the AgentCore Gateway, which hosts our agent’s tools. To learn about AgentCore Gateway and how to set up auth with Gateway, read the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway Documentation&lt;/a&gt;. Temporal policies use &lt;a href="https://dogwood-policy.github.io/dogwood/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dogwood&lt;/a&gt;, a new open-source governance language designed for agents and their tools. Dogwood supports evaluating existing &lt;a href="https://docs.cedarpolicy.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cedar policies&lt;/a&gt; and enables support for temporal conditions. Because Dogwood is compatible with existing Cedar policies, customers can continue to use their current Cedar policies without needing to migrate. For additional detail on Dogwood and its semantics, you can read the &lt;a href="https://dogwood-policy.github.io/dogwood/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;language documentation&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/opensource/introducing-dogwood-runtime-verification-for-ai-agents/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;this blog post&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The compliance team requires the following temporal controls before the agent reaches production:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent must pull the client profile, then load the portfolio, before any trade executes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;code&gt;portfolio_id&lt;/code&gt; used in a trade must exactly match the output from &lt;code&gt;get_client_profile&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Market prices must be retrieved within 1 minute of a trade execution.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;No single session can exceed $60,000 in total trade value.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Any individual trade over $25,000 requires advisor approval, one approval per trade.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent cannot buy and then sell the same security within the same trajectory if it sells for a loss.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After 15 minutes without advisor interaction, the agent loses access to write operations.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="request-flow-through-gateway-and-policy"&gt;Request flow through gateway and policy&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21661-1.png" alt="Request flow showing how tool calls from the portfolio agent pass through AgentCore Gateway, where the policy engine evaluates them against the trajectory state before allowing or denying access to the MCP tool" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Request flow through AgentCore Gateway and Policy&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This diagram demonstrates how requests to your gateway are intercepted and evaluated by Policy in AgentCore. When the portfolio agent initiates a tool call, the following steps occur:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The request arrives at the AgentCore Gateway. The advisor is already authenticated through AgentCore Identity. The request carries the trajectory ID for the current session.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The policy engine retrieves the trajectory’s accumulated state.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Each temporal policy evaluates the current request against that history.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If all policies permit, the request proceeds to the MCP tool. If any policy forbids, the request is denied and the denial is logged.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On successful execution, the action and its result are appended to the trajectory state for future evaluations.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementing-temporal-policies"&gt;Implementing temporal policies&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you have an existing policy engine in &lt;code&gt;ENFORCE&lt;/code&gt; mode, you can either update its enforcement mode to &lt;code&gt;LOG_ONLY&lt;/code&gt;, or you can change the enforcement mode of the individual policies. Switching existing policies or policy engines to &lt;code&gt;LOG_ONLY&lt;/code&gt; mode is not recommended for production workloads since policies will no longer enforce those security rules.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before implementing this solution, verify that you have met the following prerequisites:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An active AWS account with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore enabled.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AgentCore Gateway with at least one MCP target configured.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A policy engine attached to the gateway.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Appropriate Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions to create and manage policy resources (see documentation).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-1-workflow-sequencing-multi-hop-chain"&gt;Policy 1: Workflow sequencing (multi-hop chain)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The compliance team requires that the agent follow &lt;code&gt;get_client_profile&lt;/code&gt;, then &lt;code&gt;load_portfolio&lt;/code&gt;, then &lt;code&gt;rebalance_portfolio&lt;/code&gt; in sequence. Without the client profile, the agent has no system-verified context about which portfolios belong to this client, what the client’s risk tolerance is, or what account restrictions apply.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;permit (principal, action == AgentCore::Action::"FinTarget___load_portfolio", resource == AgentCore::Gateway::&amp;lt;GATEWAY_ARN&amp;gt;)
when temporal {
    formerly within 5m (AgentCore::Action::"FinTarget___get_client_profile"::response{eventResource: resource})
};

permit (principal, action == AgentCore::Action::"FinTarget___rebalance_portfolio", resource == AgentCore::Gateway::&amp;lt;GATEWAY_ARN&amp;gt;)
when temporal {
    formerly within 5m (AgentCore::Action::"FinTarget___load_portfolio"::response{eventResource: resource})
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This policy forbids &lt;code&gt;rebalance_portfolio&lt;/code&gt; unless &lt;code&gt;get_client_profile&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;load_portfolio&lt;/code&gt; have both completed in the correct order within this trajectory. An agent that skips the load profile step and jumps directly to rebalancing is denied regardless of what instructions it received.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trajectory state&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Action attempted&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Empty&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;rebalance_portfolio (portfolio_id: ” 8821”, amount: 15000)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;get_client_profile completed&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;rebalance_portfolio (portfolio_id: ” 8821”, amount: 15000)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;get_client_profile then load_portfolio completed&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;rebalance_portfolio(portfolio_id: ” 8821”, amount: 15000)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALLOW&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-2-output-to-input-integrity"&gt;Policy 2: Output-to-input integrity&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;portfolio_id&lt;/code&gt; passed to &lt;code&gt;execute_trade&lt;/code&gt; must exactly match one of the portfolio IDs returned by &lt;code&gt;get_client_profile&lt;/code&gt;. The agent cannot fabricate or substitute a different portfolio ID.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;permit (
    principal,
    action == AgentCore::Action::"execute_trade",
    resource
)
when temporal {
    formerly within 24h (
        AgentCore::Action::"get_client_profile"::response{
            input.profile_id: context.input.profile_id,
            eventResource: resource
        }
    )
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This policy prevents an attacker from using prompt injection to steer the agent to trade against a different client’s portfolio. The attacker can convince the LLM to use a fabricated ID, but the policy verifies the value against what the CRM system actually returned.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;get_client_profile returned&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;execute_trade portfolio_id&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;port-8821&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;port-8821&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALLOW&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;port-8821&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;port-3347&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-3-data-freshness"&gt;Policy 3: Data freshness&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;code&gt;get_market_price&lt;/code&gt; call must have completed within the last 30 seconds before &lt;code&gt;execute_trade&lt;/code&gt; is authorized. The agent cannot act on stale quotes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;permit (
    principal,
    action == AgentCore::Action::"execute_trade",
    resource
)
when temporal {
    formerly within 30s (
        AgentCore::Action::"get_market_price"::response{eventResource: resource}
    )
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In volatile markets, even a 60-second-old quote can represent significant price drift. This policy forces the agent to refresh its market data before every trade, ensuring that decisions are based on current information.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time since get_market_price&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Action&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4 seconds ago&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;BUY Stock A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALLOW&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2 minutes ago&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;BUY Stock A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Never called&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;BUY Stock A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-4-cumulative-budget-cap-per-trajectory"&gt;Policy 4: Cumulative budget cap per trajectory&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Total trade value in a single policy session (or trajectory) cannot exceed $60,000. This contains blast radius from runaway agents or successful attacks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;permit (
    principal,
    action == AgentCore::Action::"execute_trade",
    resource
)
when temporal {
    exists (total: Long). ((sum amount for (amount: Long), (t: Timepoint). where (formerly within 24h (
        AgentCore::Action::"get_market_price"::request{input.cost: amount, eventResource: resource} &amp;amp;&amp;amp; tp(t)))) == total &amp;amp;&amp;amp; total &amp;lt; 60000
    )
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A compromised agent executing dozens of small trades that individually look fine can still accumulate catastrophic exposure. After $60,000, all trades are denied until a new trajectory begins.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prior cumulative trades&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Current trade amount&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Total&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$15,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$15,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allow&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$15,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$22,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$37,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allow&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$37,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$30,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$67,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-5-human-approval-for-large-trades-one-time-consumption"&gt;Policy 5: Human approval for large trades (one-time consumption)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Any trade exceeding $25,000 requires the advisor’s explicit approval. Each approval is consumed by a single trade. A second large trade requires a fresh approval.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;permit (
    principal,
    action == AgentCore::Action::"execute_trade",
    resource
)
when {
    context.input.cost &amp;lt; 25000 || temporal {
        !(
            AgentCore::Action::"execute_trade"::response{eventResource: resource}
        )
        since within 24h (
            AgentCore::Action::"approve_trade"::response{ input.status: "approved", eventResource: resource}
        )
    }
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This prevents the agent from interpreting a single approval as blanket permission for multiple large trades. Each approval covers exactly one execution.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trade amount&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Approval in trajectory&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$15,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;None&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Allow (below threshold)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$30,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;None&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$30,000&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Approved (unconsumed)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALLOW&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$30,000 (second trade)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Only prior approval (consumed)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-6-mutual-exclusion"&gt;Policy 6: Mutual exclusion&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent cannot buy and then sell the same security within the same trajectory if it sells for a loss.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;permit (
    principal,
    action == AgentCore::Action::"execute_sell",
    resource
)
unless {
    context.input.profit &amp;lt; 0 &amp;amp;&amp;amp; temporal {
        formerly within 24h (
            AgentCore::Action::"execute_buy"{
                stock_symbol: context.input.stock_symbol, eventResource: resource}
        )
    }
}
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the agent sold AAPL two minutes ago and now tries to buy AAPL, the request is denied. The contradiction itself is the signal that something has gone wrong and the session should be reviewed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prior action&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Current action&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time gap&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SELL Stock A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;BUY Stock A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2 min&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SELL Stock A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;BUY Stock A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;7 min&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALLOW&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SELL Stock A&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;BUY Stock B&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2 min&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALLOW (different security)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-7-progressive-trust-decay"&gt;Policy 7: Progressive trust decay&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After 15 minutes without advisor interaction, the agent loses access to write operations (&lt;code&gt;execute_trade&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;rebalance_portfolio&lt;/code&gt;). The advisor can re-engage at any time to restore full access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;permit (
    principal,
    action in [
        AgentCore::Action::"execute_trade",
        AgentCore::Action::"rebalance_portfolio"
    ],
    resource == AgentCore::Gateway::"&amp;lt;arn&amp;gt;"
)
unless temporal {
    formerly within 15m AgentCore::Action::"interact_advisor"::response{eventResource: resource}
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the advisor walks away, the agent naturally converges toward read-only behavior. This ensures that extended autonomous operation does not accumulate unchecked risk.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time since last advisor interaction&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Action attempted&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;execute_trade&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALLOW&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;20 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;execute_trade&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DENY&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;20 minutes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;get_market_price&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ALLOW (read-only)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cost-considerations"&gt;Cost considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You only pay for the authorization requests performed during agent execution. Each time an agent calls a tool through AgentCore Gateway, Policy checks the action against your rules to determine whether it is allowed or denied. Your first 100 temporal policies per policy engine are included in the existing per-authorization-request price (see the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore pricing page&lt;/a&gt; for details).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, remove the resources you created in this walkthrough. Delete the resources in order: first delete the temporal policies from the policy engine, then detach the policy engine from the gateway, and then delete the policy engine itself. A policy engine cannot be deleted while it still contains policies or remains attached to a gateway. If you created the gateway, its MCP target solely for this walkthrough, delete those as well. Note that deleting or changing policies invalidates any active policy sessions, so perform cleanup only after your test sessions are complete.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;List and delete the policies on the policy engine. Repeat the delete-policy command for each of the seven policies:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control list-policies $
  --policy-engine-id &amp;lt;POLICY_ENGINE_ID&amp;gt;

aws bedrock-agentcore-control delete-policy $
  --policy-engine-id &amp;lt;POLICY_ENGINE_ID&amp;gt; $
  --policy-id &amp;lt;POLICY_ID&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Detach the policy engine from the gateway by updating the gateway without a policy engine configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-gateway $
  --name my-gateway $
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/my-gateway-service-role $
  --protocol-type MCP $
  --authorizer-type CUSTOM_JWT $
  --authorizer-configuration '{
    "customJWTAuthorizer": {
      "discoveryUrl": "https://cognito-idp.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/some-user-pool/.well-known/openid-configuration",
      "allowedClients": ["clientId"]
    }
  }'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Delete the policy engine:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-code"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control delete-policy-engine $
  --policy-engine-id &amp;lt;POLICY_ENGINE_ID&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;(Optional) Delete the gateway target and gateway if you created them for this walkthrough:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control delete-gateway-target $
  --gateway-identifier &amp;lt;GATEWAY_ID&amp;gt; $
  --target-id &amp;lt;TARGET_ID&amp;gt;

aws bedrock-agentcore-control delete-gateway $
  --gateway-identifier &amp;lt;GATEWAY_ID&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learned how temporal policies bring stateful, trajectory-aware authorization to agentic AI systems. You applied seven policy patterns to a hypothetical private banking portfolio agent. These patterns covered workflow sequencing, output-to-input integrity, data freshness, cumulative budget caps, human-in-the-loop approvals, mutual exclusion, and progressive trust decay. These patterns generalize across domains where agents interact with sensitive tools at runtime. Because enforcement happens at the AgentCore Gateway perimeter, outside the agent’s own reasoning loop, these protections remain tamper-proof regardless of model behavior. This gives you a declarative, auditable way to enforce operational boundaries without constraining the flexibility that makes agents valuable. To get started, review the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-create-policies.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Configure rate limits for AI traffic on AgentCore gateway</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/configure-rate-limits-for-ai-traffic-on-agentcore-gateway/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Anagh Agrawal]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 06 Aug 2026 17:50:42 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">16e424b361964bece2ce5179f108a5d8ce975e0e</guid>

					<description>Learn how to configure rate limits on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore gateway to enforce per-user and per-target traffic controls. Define request, token, and connection limits scoped by JWT claims or IAM identity to protect downstream models, tools, and agents from traffic spikes.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore gateway&lt;/a&gt; is a fully managed, serverless AI gateway that provides a single, secure entry point for &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-supported-targets.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AI traffic&lt;/a&gt;. AgentCore gateway routes traffic to tools such as &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/01-gateway/01-attach-targets/mcp/connectors/websearch" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;managed web search&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/introducing-amazon-bedrock-managed-knowledge-base-for-faster-more-accurate-enterprise-ai-applications/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;managed knowledge bases&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/01-gateway/01-attach-targets/mcp" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MCP servers&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/01-gateway/01-attach-targets/llm-inference" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;inference models&lt;/a&gt; (LLMs), &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/01-gateway/01-attach-targets/http/agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agents&lt;/a&gt; (&lt;a href="https://github.com/a2aproject/A2A" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;A2A&lt;/a&gt;, agents as tools, etc.), or &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/01-gateway/01-attach-targets/http" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HTTP&lt;/a&gt; endpoint. Today, we are announcing support for rate limiting on AgentCore gateway, giving you fine-grained control over how much traffic individual users can consume through your gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-rate-limits.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Rate limiting&lt;/a&gt; in AgentCore gateway gives you per-user control over how users consume your tools, inference models, and agents. Define OAuth or IAM-based rules for requests per minute, concurrent connections, and token throughput, making sure downstream services remain available under heavy traffic spikes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="centralized-rate-limiting-for-ai-traffic-with-agentcore-gateway"&gt;Centralized rate limiting for AI traffic with AgentCore gateway&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore gateway provides three target types: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-targets-mcp.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MCP&lt;/a&gt; targets, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-targets-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;inference&lt;/a&gt; targets, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-targets-http.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HTTP&lt;/a&gt; passthrough targets. The following rate limiting metrics are supported on the targets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Request rate limits, measured in requests per second (RPS) and requests per minute (RPM), apply to all target types. Each limit defines a maximum count of requests permitted within the given time window, and the gateway measures every incoming request against it. A request counts as exactly one unit toward the configured limit, regardless of how long it takes to complete, a request that finishes in 50 milliseconds and one that streams for 90 seconds each consume exactly one unit from the per-second or per-minute limit.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Token rate limits&lt;/strong&gt;, measured in tokens per minute (TPM), apply to inference targets only. Token rate limiting accounts for both input tokens and output tokens. The full round-trip token cost of a request counts against the limit. AgentCore gateway uses a general-purpose tokenizer to estimate the incoming tokens for a request and deducts it from the rate-limit bucket upfront before the gateway dispatches the inference call. Once the inference call returns a response, which includes actual input and output token usage reported by the model provider, the gateway reconciles the limit by accounting for the true token consumption.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Connection rate limits&lt;/strong&gt;, measured in connections per second (CPS), apply to all target types. Unlike request rate limits, connection rate limiting tracks how long each request holds an open connection. For example, if a streaming inference call takes 100 seconds to complete, that request consumes one connection slot for the entire duration. CPS provides an additional mechanism for protecting targets against long-lived concurrent sessions particularly useful when you need to cap how many simultaneous connections a target sustains, rather than how many requests arrive in each window.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For this use case, assume three user groups: Basic, Advanced, and Beta. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt; handles inbound authentication using &lt;a href="https://www.jwt.io/introduction#what-is-json-web-token" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JSON Web Tokens&lt;/a&gt; (JWT) with &lt;a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/business/identity-access/microsoft-entra-id" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Microsoft Entra ID&lt;/a&gt; as the identity provider and also serves as the token vending service for outbound targets. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; enforces role-based access control (RBAC), scoping each group’s access to specific targets and models. The following diagram illustrates this configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21567-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing AgentCore gateway with three user groups (Basic, Advanced, Beta) connecting through AgentCore Identity and Policy to MCP targets, inference targets, and HTTP targets" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: AgentCore gateway rate limiting architecture with user groups, identity, and policy enforcement&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Basic users operate under more restrictive rate limits than Advanced users, while Beta users receive elevated limits on restricted models, enabling the organization to benchmark performance and suitability before rolling these models out to the broader organization. Before setting up rate limits for each user-group, review the rate limit structure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="rate-limit-structure"&gt;Rate limit structure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A rate limit configuration consists of two parts: dimension keys and entries. Dimension keys define how the gateway groups incoming traffic into rate buckets. Entries define the allowed throughput for each bucket.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we use the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt; to create the rate limit configuration. The following example demonstrates the relationship between dimension keys and entries. This rate limit uses &lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt; as the dimension key and defines two entries: a specific entry for the &lt;code&gt;Booking&lt;/code&gt; target (MCP server), a high-traffic target, at 100 requests per second, and a wildcard entry that applies 10 requests per second individually to each remaining target, meaning every other target receives its own 10 RPS bucket.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21567-2.png" alt="Rate limit structure showing targetName as the dimension key with a specific entry for Booking at 100 RPS and a wildcard entry at 10 RPS for all other targets" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Rate limit structure with dimension keys and entries&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dimension keys define how the gateway groups traffic into rate buckets. When a request arrives, the gateway resolves each dimension key to its value from the request context and uses the resulting combination to assign the request to the correct rate bucket. AgentCore gateway supports the following dimension keys: &lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;toolName&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;qualifiedModelId&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.&amp;lt;claim&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;$.context.iam.principal&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;$.context.iam.sourceIdentity&lt;/code&gt;. We will explore each of these through examples in the sections that follow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Entries are the rules within a rate limit. Each entry specifies a set of dimension keys to match, and the allowed throughput for that match. Entries support the special catch-all default value * that gives each distinct value its own independent bucket at the configured rate. When the gateway evaluates a request, it checks whether an entry matches by name before falling back to the wildcard. A named entry takes precedence because it refers to the value explicitly rather than relying on the catch-all.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Taking the preceding rate limit as an example, when a request arrives for the &lt;code&gt;Booking&lt;/code&gt; target (MCP server), the gateway matches the first entry and allows up to 100 RPS. This entry takes precedence because the most specific value match wins over default value * as it refers to the &lt;code&gt;Booking&lt;/code&gt; target by name. For any other target, no named entry exists, so the gateway falls back to the wildcard entry and allows up to 10 requests per second. Each target that matches the wildcard (&lt;code&gt;Docs&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;BedrockMantle&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;CustomPlatform&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;awsdocsagent&lt;/code&gt;) gets its own independent bucket.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can combine multiple dimension keys for more granular control. For example, dimensionKeys: [“&lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”] groups traffic by both target and caller identity role claim, giving each user-group (Basic, Advanced, or Beta in the preceding example) their own independent rate bucket per target.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="types-of-rate-limits-and-example-configurations"&gt;Types of rate limits and example configurations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore gateway enforces two layers of rate limiting: customer-defined rate limits and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/general/latest/gr/aws_service_limits.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Service Quotas&lt;/a&gt;. Customer-defined rate limits are evaluated first. If the request passes, service quotas are evaluated. The following sections explain service quotas and the different types of customer-defined rate limits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Service managed quotas&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These are the limits enforced on AgentCore gateway per AWS account by the service. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/bedrock-agentcore-limits.html#gateway-endpoints-quotas" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Service managed quotas&lt;/a&gt; define the ceiling that customer-defined rate limits cannot exceed. The effective rate for requests is the minimum of the customer-defined limit and the service-managed limit. You can request increases for some quotas using the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/servicequotas" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Service Quotas console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="2" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customer-defined&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;strong&gt;user limits&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;User-level limits use &lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.&amp;lt;claim&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;$.context.iam.principal&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;$.context.iam.sourceIdentity&lt;/code&gt; as the dimension keys to control how much traffic individual users or entire user-group can consume. These limits enforce fair usage across your caller base and prevent any single caller from monopolizing gateway capacity. The following example assigns different request rates per user group. The JWT role claim is an array, so each unique combination requires its own entry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-gateway-rate-limit \
  --gateway-identifier my-gateway-abc1234567 \
  --dimension-keys '["$.context.jwt.role"]' \
  --description "Per-role request and connection limit" \
  --entries '[
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Basic\"]"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 100, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 50, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Advanced\"]"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 300, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 150, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Advanced\", \"Beta\"]"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 300, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 200, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "*"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 80, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 10, "period": "second"}]
    }
  ]'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this configuration, Basic users receive two buckets, 100 RPM and 50 CPS, meaning every request from any Basic user counts toward the same 100 RPM total, and every connection counts toward the same 50 CPS total. If one Basic user sends 80 requests in a minute, only 20 remain for all other Basic users in that window. Advanced users receive their own two buckets at 300 RPM and 150 CPS, governed by the same collective behavior. Users with [“Advanced”, “Beta”] group membership receive two buckets at 300 RPM and 200 CPS. The higher connection allowance accommodates their streaming-heavy benchmarking workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;However, within a group, a single user can still consume the entire group rate bucket, throttling everyone else in that group. For example, one Basic user sending 100 requests in a minute would leave zero capacity for all other Basic users. To prevent this, we create the following rate limit configuration as well.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-gateway-rate-limit \
  --gateway-identifier my-gateway-abc1234567 \
  --dimension-keys '["$.context.jwt.role", "$.context.jwt.sub"]' \
  --description "Per-user request and connection limit within each role" \
  --entries '[
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Basic\"]", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 20, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 10, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Advanced\"]", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 60, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 30, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Advanced\", \"Beta\"]", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 60, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 50, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "*", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 20, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 20, "period": "second"}]
    }
  ]'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this configuration, each individual user is capped at their own rate regardless of how many users exist in their group. The &lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.sub&lt;/code&gt; claim from the JWT uniquely identifies each user, enabling the gateway to track and enforce limits at the individual level. Even if the group-level limit allows 100 RPM total for Basic, no single user can consume more than 20 RPM and 10 CPS of that shared pool. The same logic applies to Advanced and Beta users at their respective individual caps. Together, the per-group limit and the per-user limit create a two-layer enforcement model: the group ceiling helps prevent one group from starving another, and the per-user ceiling helps prevent one individual from starving their peers within the same group.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both rate limits are evaluated independently using AND semantics. A request must pass both the group-level limit and the per-user limit to proceed. If either check denies the request, the gateway returns a throttling response. For example, if Arnav (Basic) has consumed 20 RPM individually, his next request is denied by the per-user limit even though the Basic group still has 80 RPM of remaining capacity. Conversely, if the Basic group has collectively consumed 100 RPM, all Basic users are throttled regardless of their individual consumption.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customer defined target-level limits&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Target-level limits use &lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;qualifiedModelId&lt;/code&gt;, or &lt;code&gt;toolName&lt;/code&gt; as the dimension key to control throughput to specific downstream targets, models, or tools. These limits protect backend capacity and distribute load across your target resources. The following example limits traffic on a per-target basis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-gateway-rate-limit \
  --gateway-identifier my-gateway-abc1234567 \
  --dimension-keys '["targetName"]' \
  --description "Per-target rate limit" \
  --entries '[
    {
      "dimensions": {"targetName": "Booking"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 20, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"targetName": "Docs"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 15, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"targetName": "awsdocsagent"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 10, "period": "second"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 60, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"targetName": "BedrockMantle"},
      "tokens": [{"rate": 100000, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 250, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"targetName": "CustomPlatform"},
      "tokens": [{"rate": 50000, "period": "minute"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 100, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"targetName": "*"},
      "tokens": [{"rate": 10000, "period": "minute"}],
      "requests": [{"rate": 10, "period": "second"}],
      "connections": [{"rate": 50, "period": "second"}]
    }
  ]'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also use &lt;code&gt;qualifiedModelId&lt;/code&gt; to set connection rate limits (CPS) per model, or &lt;code&gt;toolName&lt;/code&gt; to set request rate limits (RPS) per individual tool such as &lt;code&gt;Booking___bookTool&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;Docs___searchDocsTool&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Note: We exclude customer-defined target-level rate limits from our use-case configuration. Beta users run heavy benchmarking workloads against restricted models, consuming a disproportionate share of a shared target-level limit. Because this limit dimensions only on &lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;, all users share a single ceiling, meaning high traffic from one group or individual can starve everyone else on that target. When a subset of users is expected to dominate token or connection consumption on a specific target, scope the limit by identity instead (for example, [“&lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”]). See the following example.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customer defined target-user level limits.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Hybrid limits combine target and user dimensions in a single rate limit configuration, giving you the most granular control. Using multi-dimension keys, you can scope rate limits to a specific user or user group on a specific target, model, or tool.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example enforces token limits at the model level, scoped to each user within their group. The &lt;code&gt;qualifiedModelId&lt;/code&gt; dimension is the fully qualified model identifier for inference targets. It uniquely identifies the model being invoked (see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-rate-limits-dimensions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documentation&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-gateway-rate-limit \
  --gateway-identifier my-gateway-abc1234567 \
  --dimension-keys '["$.context.jwt.role", "qualifiedModelId", "$.context.jwt.sub"]' \
  --description "Per-user per-role per-model TPM and access limit" \
  --entries '[
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Advanced\", \"Beta\"]", "qualifiedModelId": "anthropic.claude-fable-5", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "tokens": [{"rate": 80000, "period": "minute"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Basic\"]", "qualifiedModelId": "anthropic.claude-fable-5", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 0, "period": "second"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Advanced\"]", "qualifiedModelId": "anthropic.claude-fable-5", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 0, "period": "second"}]
    },
    ... (repeat for openai.gpt-5.6-luna and openai.gpt-5.6-terra)
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Advanced\"]", "qualifiedModelId": "*", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "tokens": [{"rate": 40000, "period": "minute"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.role": "[\"Basic\"]", "qualifiedModelId": "*", "$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "tokens": [{"rate": 20000, "period": "minute"}]
    }
  ]'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this configuration, anthropic.claude-fable-5 is a restricted model. Only users with the [“Advanced”, “Beta”] role can invoke it, receiving 80,000 TPM per user for benchmarking and evaluation workloads. Both [“Basic”] and [“Advanced”] users are blocked* from invoking this model with a rate of zero. The same pattern applies to other restricted models (openai.gpt-5.6-luna and openai.gpt-5.6-terra), make sure to add entries following the same structure for each. For generally available models, Basic users receive 20,000 TPM per user while Advanced users receive 40,000 TPM per user through wildcard entries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Specific entries take precedence over wildcards following the most-specific-match-wins rule. When María (sub: “María”, role: [“Advanced”, “Beta”]) invokes anthropic.claude-fable-5, the gateway matches the explicit entry and applies 80,000 TPM scoped to María individually. If María exhausts 80,000 TPM limit, other Beta users remain unaffected because * on &lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.sub&lt;/code&gt; gives each user their own isolated bucket. When John (sub: “John”, role: [“Advanced”]) attempts anthropic.claude-fable-5, the gateway matches the explicit [“Advanced”] entry for that model, which sets requests to zero blocking the call. When John invokes a generally available model like anthropic.claude-sonnet-5, no explicit entry exists for that model-role combination, so the gateway falls through to the wildcard entry for [“Advanced”] and applies 40,000 TPM. When Arnav (sub: “Arnav”, role: [“Basic”]) invokes the same generally available model, he receives 40,000 TPM through the Basic wildcard entry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can also combine dimensions such as [“&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;”] for per-role per-target request limits, [“&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.sub&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;”] for per-user per-target combined request and token limits, or [“&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;toolName&lt;/code&gt;”] for per-role per-tool request limits. For more rate limiting configurations, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-rate-limits-examples.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Rate limit API examples&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="rate-limits-for-agentic-workloads"&gt;Rate limits for agentic workloads&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider the following AgentCore gateway configuration where a user invokes the AWS Documentation Agent. The agent uses two downstream resources through the gateway: the &lt;code&gt;Docs&lt;/code&gt; MCP target for document search and retrieval, and the &lt;code&gt;BedrockMantle&lt;/code&gt; inference target for reasoning through the anthropic.claude-sonnet-5 model. The following architecture shows this:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21567-3.png" alt="Architecture showing a user invoking the AWS Documentation Agent through AgentCore gateway, with the agent calling both the Docs MCP target and BedrockMantle inference target" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Agentic workload rate limiting with downstream resource consumption&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are two types of rate limits to consider for agentic workloads:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Rate limits on agent invocation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first type protects how frequently users or other services can invoke the agent. These are request (RPM) and connection (CPS) limits scoped to the agent target itself. At the simplest level, you can dimension on &lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt; alone, for example, {“&lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;”: “awsdocsagent”}, so that all users share a single invocation ceiling. For more granular control, pair &lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt; with user dimensions such as [“&lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”] or [“&lt;code&gt;targetName&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.sub&lt;/code&gt;”] to cap how often each user group or individual user can invoke the agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Rate limits on resources consumed by the agent.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The second type protects the downstream resources the agent consumes on each invocation. AWS Documentation Agent triggers multiple downstream requests. The agent calls the &lt;code&gt;Docs&lt;/code&gt; MCP target for document retrieval and the &lt;code&gt;BedrockMantle&lt;/code&gt; target for inference. How you rate limit these downstream calls depends on how the agent authenticates with the gateway when invoking those resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the agent performs an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/on-behalf-of-token-exchange.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;on-behalf-of&lt;/a&gt; (OBO) token exchange based on the user’s incoming JWT token, then the downstream requests carry the original user’s identity. All existing user-based rate limits apply. The per-role and per-user limits you configured previously will enforce on the agent’s downstream calls as if the user made them directly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;However, if the agent performs a machine-to-machine grant to obtain a new token (for example, a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/common-use-cases.html#machine-to-machine-auth" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;client credentials&lt;/a&gt; flow), the downstream requests carry the agent’s own identity rather than the caller’s. In this case, the user-based rate limits will not match the user’s claims. You should add rate limits that identify the agent itself, based on your identity provider, use a claim that uniquely identifies the agent, such as &lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.azp&lt;/code&gt; (authorized party), and limit accordingly to prevent a single agent from exhausting shared resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="rate-limiting-best-practices"&gt;Rate limiting best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow these best practices when creating rate limits with AgentCore gateway:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If you are using Policy in AgentCore to enforce RBAC authorization, it is important to understand the evaluation order. Rate limits are applied first; AgentCore Policy is evaluated after. This means that even if a user or role is ultimately denied access by AgentCore Policy, their request still consumes the rate limit bucket before that denial occurs. To avoid this consumption, create a rate limit entry that explicitly assigns a rate of zero to users or groups that AgentCore Policy would block, this makes sure the request is rejected at the rate limit layer without depleting the budget available to authorized callers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The gateway evaluates rate limits with more dimension keys first (more specific limits take priority). Within the same number of dimensions, the gateway evaluates rate limits with tighter (lower) rates first. Evaluation short-circuits on the first denial, meaning the gateway does not evaluate remaining rate limits once a request is denied. Design your rate limit configuration so that your tightest limits live at the highest-dimension level (for example, the three-key [“&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”,“&lt;code&gt;qualifiedModelId&lt;/code&gt;”,“&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.sub&lt;/code&gt;”] limit).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Two rate limits with same dimension key but with different orders cannot be created. For example, you cannot create a rate limit with [“&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;qualifiedModelId&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.sub&lt;/code&gt;”] and [“&lt;code&gt;qualifiedModelId&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.role&lt;/code&gt;”, “&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.sub&lt;/code&gt;”] dimension key on the same gateway. However, the order of the dimension key matters. When a rate limit has multiple dimension keys, the order you declare them determines how wildcards can be used. The catch all default * can only appear in trailing positions. If you use * at position N, all subsequent positions must also be *. This trailing-only constraint facilitates predictable matching behavior. To understand this behavior, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-rate-limits-enforcement.html#gateway-rate-limits-enforcement-entry-matching" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;examples&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Avoid using high-cardinality or unbounded JWT claims as dimension keys (for example, &lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.jti&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.nonce&lt;/code&gt;, or request IDs). These create an unbounded number of rate buckets, and may reduce the effectiveness of rate limiting. Use stable, bounded identifiers such as sub, role, team, or tier instead.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The gateway uses fail-open semantics for rate limit evaluation. Because of fail-open behavior, do not rely solely on rate limits as a security boundary. Use rate limits for traffic management and quality of service, and use authentication, authorization, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-waf.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS WAF&lt;/a&gt; rules for security enforcement.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enable &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-gateway-metrics.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;application logs&lt;/a&gt; on your AgentCore gateway. This gives you access to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-rate-limits-enforcement.html#gateway-rate-limits-enforcement-tracing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;rate limiting logs&lt;/a&gt;. The gateway emits &lt;a href="https://opentelemetry.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTelemetry (OTEL)&lt;/a&gt; span attributes on the server span for every request where customer rate limits are evaluated. Use these attributes for debugging and monitoring.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Make sure to include a catch-all entry, consider a gateway with a single rate limit for simplicity:&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-gateway-rate-limit \
  --gateway-identifier my-gateway-abc1234567 \
  --dimension-keys '["$.context.jwt.sub"]' \
  --description "Per-sub request limit" \
  --entries '[
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.sub": "Arnav"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 100, "period": "minute"}]
    }
  ]'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this configuration, only Arnav has an explicit entry. If John (&lt;code&gt;$.context.jwt.sub&lt;/code&gt;: “John”) invokes the gateway, the request does not match any entry, the rate limit is effectively skipped, and John falls through to service-managed quotas with no customer-defined enforcement. Adding a wildcard catch-all entry makes sure that all callers without an explicit entry receive their own per-user rate limit bucket:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws bedrock-agentcore-control create-gateway-rate-limit \
  --gateway-identifier my-gateway-abc1234567 \
  --dimension-keys '["$.context.jwt.sub"]' \
  --description "Per-sub request limit" \
  --entries '[
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.sub": "Arnav"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 100, "period": "minute"}]
    },
    {
      "dimensions": {"$.context.jwt.sub": "*"},
      "requests": [{"rate": 50, "period": "minute"}]
    }
  ]'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now John, María, and any other caller each receive their own isolated 50 RPM bucket through the wildcard, while Arnav retains his explicit 100 RPM allocation. Without the catch-all entry, unmatched callers bypass the rate limit entirely.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more best practices, see the AgentCore gateway &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-rate-limits-best-practices.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walked through how to configure rate limits on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore gateway to govern AI traffic, enforcing fair usage across roles and users while helping to prevent any single caller or workload from exhausting shared capacity. We demonstrated how to layer rate limits: user-level limits for per-role and per-user fairness, target-level limits for protecting downstream service capacity, and multi-dimensional limits that combine user and target to enforce fine-grained rate limits. We also covered rate limiting considerations for agentic workloads, where downstream resource consumption varies depending on how the agent authenticates with the gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Rate limits are one layer of a comprehensive traffic management strategy. Combined with AgentCore Identity for authentication, Policy in AgentCore for role-based access control, and application logging for observability, they give you the tools to operate a production-grade AI gateway with confidence, facilitating fair usage, protecting backend services, and maintaining availability as your workloads scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next"&gt;What’s next?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started with rate limits on AgentCore gateway, explore the following resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add rate limits to AgentCore gateway, complete API &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore-control/latest/APIReference/API_Operations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;reference&lt;/a&gt; for creating, updating, and deleting rate limits with dimension keys, entries, and supported metrics.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Rate limit API &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-rate-limits-examples.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;examples&lt;/a&gt;, additional CLI examples covering batch operations, listing, filtering, and delete workflows.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AgentCore gateway quotas and limits, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/bedrock-agentcore-limits.html#gateway-endpoints-quotas" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;service-managed quotas&lt;/a&gt;, default limits, and how to request increases.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt;, configure inbound JWT authentication and outbound token vending for AgentCore gateway targets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, set up role-based access control to complement rate limits with authorization enforcement.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/observability-gateway-metrics.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Application logging&lt;/a&gt; for AgentCore gateway, enable OpenTelemetry-based logging to monitor rate limit evaluations, denials, and per-bucket consumption in real time.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We continue to invest in AgentCore gateway based on customer feedback. We look forward to how you use AgentCore gateway as a central enforcement system for your AI traffic.&lt;/p&gt;

   &lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-21567-7.png" alt="Sean Eichenberger" width="100" height="100"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
